Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Since the giant heads have appeared in the sky all humanity is in danger, so all Ricks and Mortys from all parallel universes are gathering in groups to find a solution to get rid of them. 

There are n parallel universes participating in this event (n Ricks and n Mortys). I. e. each of n universes has one Rick and one Morty. They're gathering in m groups. Each person can be in many groups and a group can contain an arbitrary number of members.

Ricks and Mortys have registered online in these groups. So, a person can have joined a group more than once (developer of this website hadn't considered this possibility). [Image] 

Summer from universe #1 knows that in each parallel universe (including hers) exactly one of Rick and Morty from that universe is a traitor and is loyal, but no one knows which one. She knows that we are doomed if there's a group such that every member in that group is a traitor (they will plan and destroy the world). 

Summer knows that if there's a possibility that world ends (there's a group where all members are traitors) she should immediately cancel this event. So she wants to know if she should cancel the event. You have to tell her yes if and only if there's at least one scenario (among all 2^{n} possible scenarios, 2 possible scenarios for who a traitor in each universe) such that in that scenario the world will end.


-----Input-----

The first line of input contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^4) — number of universes and number of groups respectively.

The next m lines contain the information about the groups. i-th of them first contains an integer k (number of times someone joined i-th group, k > 0) followed by k integers v_{i}, 1, v_{i}, 2, ..., v_{i}, k. If v_{i}, j is negative, it means that Rick from universe number  - v_{i}, j has joined this group and otherwise it means that Morty from universe number v_{i}, j has joined it.

Sum of k for all groups does not exceed 10^4.


-----Output-----

In a single line print the answer to Summer's question. Print "YES" if she should cancel the event and "NO" otherwise.


-----Examples-----
Input
4 2
1 -3
4 -2 3 2 -3

Output
YES

Input
5 2
5 3 -2 1 -1 5
3 -5 2 5

Output
NO

Input
7 2
3 -1 6 7
7 -5 4 2 4 7 -3 4

Output
YES



-----Note-----

In the first sample testcase, 1st group only contains the Rick from universe number 3, so in case he's a traitor, then all members of this group are traitors and so Summer should cancel the event.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

## Problem Understanding

1. There are `n` parallel universes, each with one Rick and one Morty
2. In each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor (the other is loyal)
3. We have `m` groups, where each group contains members from various universes
4. We need to determine if there exists ANY scenario where a group has ALL traitors
5. If such a scenario exists, we output "YES", otherwise "NO"

## Key Insight

For a group to have all traitors, every member in that group must be a traitor in their respective universe.

For each universe, we have two possibilities:
- Rick is traitor, Morty is loyal
- Morty is traitor, Rick is loyal

For a specific group to have all traitors, we need to check if we can assign traitor status to each member such that:
- For each member in the group, that person is the traitor in their universe
- Since each universe has exactly one traitor, we just need to ensure there's no conflict

- Universe 5: only Rick

I think I made a mistake in example 2. Let me re-read:

Group 1: 5 3 -2 1 -1 5 → Morty from 3, Rick from 2, Morty from 1, Rick from 1, Morty from 5
- Universe 1: Morty and Rick both appear
- Universe 2: Rick appears (from -2)
- Universe 3: Morty appears (from 3)
- Universe 5: Morty appears (from 5)

For universe 1: both Rick and Morty appear → invalid
For universe 2: only Rick → valid
For universe 3: only Morty → valid  
For universe 5: only Morty → valid

But since universe 1 has both, we cannot have both as traitors, so we cannot have all members as traitors.

Group 2: 3 -5 2 5 → 
- -5: Rick from 5
- 2: Morty from 2  
- 5: Morty from 5

Universe 5: both Rick and Morty appear → both appear → cannot have both as traitors → invalid

So both groups are invalid → answer should be NO.

This matches the expected output.

Therefore, the algorithm is:

For each group:
- Create a set of universes that appear
- For each universe in that group, check if both Rick and Morty appear
- If for any universe, both appear, then this group cannot have all traitors
- If for no universe do both appear, then this group can have all traitors

If any group can have all traitors, return "YES", otherwise "NO".

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

for _ in range(m):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    k = line[0]
    members = line[1:k+1]
    
    # Track which universes have Rick and which have Morty in this group
    rick_universes = set()
    morty_universes = set()
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            # Negative means Rick from universe -member
            universe = -member
            rick_universes.add(universe)
        else:
            # Positive means Morty from universe member
            universe = member
            morty_universes.add(universe)
    
    # Check if any universe has both Rick and Morty in this group
    # If no universe has both, then we can have all traitors
    has_both = False
    for universe in rick_universes:
        if universe in morty_universes:
            has_both = True
            break
    
    # If no universe has both, then this group can have all traitors
    if not has_both:
        print("YES")
        exit()

# If we get here, no group can have all traitors
print("NO")
```

- Wait, -1 means Rick from universe 1, so we have: Morty 3, Rick 2, Morty 1, Rick 1, Morty 5
- So universe 1 has both Morty 1 and Rick 1 → cannot have both as traitors → cannot have all traitors in this group
- Group 2: 3 -5 2 5 → Rick 5, Morty 2, Morty 5
- Universe 5 has both Rick 5 and Morty 5 → cannot have all traitors in this group

Actually, I think I made an error. In group 1, we have Rick from universe 1, Morty from universe 6, Morty from universe 7. No universe has both Rick and Morty, so we can make Rick from universe 1 the traitor, and Morty from 6 and 7 the traitors. This works.

So algorithm:
For each group:
- Create a set of universes that have either Rick or Morty in that group
- For each universe in that group, check if both Rick and Morty are present
- If any universe has both Rick and Morty in this group, this group cannot have all traitors
- If no universe has both Rick and Morty, then this group can have all traitors in some scenario

We only need to find if there exists at least one such group.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Process each group
found_valid_group = False

for _ in range(m):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    k = line[0]
    members = line[1:]
    
    # Track which universes have their Rick or Morty in this group
    # For each universe, we need to know if both Rick and Morty are present
    universe_has_ritc = set()
    universe_has_morty = set()
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            # Negative means Rick from universe -member
            universe = -member
            universe_has_ritc.add(universe)
        else:
            # Positive means Morty from universe member
            universe = member
            universe_has_morty.add(universe)
    
    # Check if any universe has both Rick and Morty
    has_both = False
    for universe in universe_has_ritc:
        if universe in universe_has_morty:
            has_both = True
            break
    
    # If no universe has both, this group can have all traitors
    if not has_both:
        found_valid_group = True
        break

if found_valid_group:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Since the giant heads have appeared in the sky all humanity is in danger, so all Ricks and Mortys from all parallel universes are gathering in groups to find a solution to get rid of them. 

There are n parallel universes participating in this event (n Ricks and n Mortys). I. e. each of n universes has one Rick and one Morty. They're gathering in m groups. Each person can be in many groups and a group can contain an arbitrary number of members.

Ricks and Mortys have registered online in these groups. So, a person can have joined a group more than once (developer of this website hadn't considered this possibility). [Image] 

Summer from universe #1 knows that in each parallel universe (including hers) exactly one of Rick and Morty from that universe is a traitor and is loyal, but no one knows which one. She knows that we are doomed if there's a group such that every member in that group is a traitor (they will plan and destroy the world). 

Summer knows that if there's a possibility that world ends (there's a group where all members are traitors) she should immediately cancel this event. So she wants to know if she should cancel the event. You have to tell her yes if and only if there's at least one scenario (among all 2^{n} possible scenarios, 2 possible scenarios for who a traitor in each universe) such that in that scenario the world will end.


-----Input-----

The first line of input contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^4) — number of universes and number of groups respectively.

The next m lines contain the information about the groups. i-th of them first contains an integer k (number of times someone joined i-th group, k > 0) followed by k integers v_{i}, 1, v_{i}, 2, ..., v_{i}, k. If v_{i}, j is negative, it means that Rick from universe number  - v_{i}, j has joined this group and otherwise it means that Morty from universe number v_{i}, j has joined it.

Sum of k for all groups does not exceed 10^4.


-----Output-----

In a single line print the answer to Summer's question. Print "YES" if she should cancel the event and "NO" otherwise.


-----Examples-----
Input
4 2
1 -3
4 -2 3 2 -3

Output
YES

Input
5 2
5 3 -2 1 -1 5
3 -5 2 5

Output
NO

Input
7 2
3 -1 6 7
7 -5 4 2 4 7 -3 4

Output
YES



-----Note-----

In the first sample testcase, 1st group only contains the Rick from universe number 3, so in case he's a traitor, then all members of this group are traitors and so Summer should cancel the event.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` parallel universes, each with one Rick and one Morty
2. In each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor (the other is loyal)
3. People can join groups multiple times
4. The world ends if there exists a group where ALL members are traitors
5. We need to determine if there exists ANY scenario where the world ends

## Key Insight

The world will end if there exists a group where all members are traitors. For a group to have all traitors, we need to check if there's a way to assign traitors such that every member in that group is a traitor.

Since in each universe exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor, we need to check if we can choose traitors for each universe such that in at least one group, all members are traitors.

## Approach

A member from universe `u` can be a traitor if:
- The member is Rick (negative number) → we choose Rick as traitor in universe `-v`
- The member is Morty (positive number) → we choose Morty as traitor in universe `v`

For a group to have all traitors, we need that for each universe involved, we can choose the traitor to be the specific person who appears in the group.

The constraint is: for each universe, we can only have one traitor (either Rick or Morty).

So for a group to have all traitors, we need that in each universe that appears in the group, the members are not conflicting. Since each universe appears at most once in terms of who the traitor can be, we need to check if we can assign traitors consistently.

If only Rick appears, we must choose Rick as traitor.
If only Morty appears, we must choose Morty as traitor.

So for a group to be all traitors, in each universe involved, either:
- Only one person appears (Rick or Morty), and we choose that person as traitor
- Or the group contains both Rick and Morty from the same universe, which is impossible

- Group 1: only Rick from universe 3 → possible (choose Rick from universe 3 as traitor)
- Group 2: Rick from 2, Morty from 3, Morty from 2, Rick from 3 → universe 2 appears with both Rick and Morty → impossible

So only group 1 works. Therefore answer is YES.

1. For each group, check if it's possible for all members to be traitors

Example 3:
Group 1: 3 -1 6 7
- -1: Rick from universe 1
- 6: Morty from universe 6  
- 7: Morty from universe 7

Universe 1: only Rick → ok
Universe 6: only Morty → ok
Universe 7: only Morty → ok

No conflicts → this group can have all traitors → answer YES.

So algorithm:
For each group:
- Create a set of universes that appear in it
- For each universe in the group, check if both Rick and Morty appear
- If any universe has both Rick and Morty, skip this group
- If no universe has both, then this group can have all traitors → answer is YES

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

should_cancel = False

for _ in range(m):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    k = line[0]
    members = line[1:]
    
    # Check if this group can have all members as traitors
    # For each universe, check if both Rick and Morty appear
    universe_types = {}  # universe -> set of types (rick or morty)
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            universe = -member
            type_ = "rick"
        else:
            universe = member
            type_ = "morty"
        
        if universe not in universe_types:
            universe_types[universe] = set()
        universe_types[universe].add(type_)
    
    # Check if any universe has both rick and morty
    valid_group = True
    for universe, types in universe_types.items():
        if "rick" in types and "morty" in types:
            valid_group = False
            break
    
    if valid_group:
        should_cancel = True
        break

print("YES" if should_cancel else "NO")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` parallel universes, each with one Rick and one Morty
2. In each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor (the other is loyal)
3. We have `m` groups where people can join (a person can join multiple groups)
4. The world ends if there's ANY group where ALL members are traitors
5. We need to determine if there exists ANY scenario (among 2^n possibilities) where the world ends

## Key Insight

The world ends if there's a group where all members are traitors. For a group to have all traitors, for each member in that group:
- If it's Rick from universe `i`, then Rick from universe `i` must be the traitor
- If it's Morty from universe `i`, then Morty from universe `i` must be the traitor

For a group to have all traitors, we need to check if there's a consistent assignment of traitors across all members of that group.

## Critical Observation

For a group to have all traitors, the traitor assignments must be consistent. This means:
- For each member in the group, we must choose the traitor from their universe
- The traitors from different members in the same group must not contradict each other

Since in each universe exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor, we need to check if we can assign traitors such that for a specific group, all members are traitors.

For a group to allow all members to be traitors, the constraints from different members must be satisfiable.

**Key insight**: A group can have all traitors if and only if there's no conflict in the required traitor assignments from different members.

More specifically: For a group with members from different universes, if we have both a Rick and a Morty from the same universe in the group, then we have a contradiction because we can't have both Rick and Morty as traitors in the same universe.

So a group can have all traitors if and only if it doesn't contain both a Rick and a Morty from the same universe.

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, the constraint is that in each universe exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor. So if a group contains both a Rick and a Morty from universe `i`, then we cannot have both of them as traitors (since only one can be a traitor in that universe). 

Therefore, for a group to have ALL members as traitors, it must not contain both a Rick and a Morty from the same universe.

Actually, let me think again. If a group contains both a Rick and a Morty from universe `i`, then it's impossible for both to be traitors (since only one can be a traitor in that universe). So for the group to have all traitors, this situation cannot happen.

Therefore, a group can have all traitors if and only if it doesn't contain both a Rick and a Morty from the same universe.

Even more specifically: if a group contains both a Rick and a Morty from the same universe, then it's impossible for all members to be traitors (because we can't have both as traitors).

If a group contains only Ricks from different universes, or only Mortys from different universes, then we can assign traitors consistently - we just make the person in the group the traitor.

Therefore, we just need to check if there exists any group that contains members from only one type (either all Ricks or all Mortys) with no conflicts in universes.

Actually, let me re-express: For a group to have all traitors, we need to assign traitors such that for each member in the group, they are the traitor. This is only possible if for each universe, we don't have both a Rick and a Morty from that universe in the group.

So the answer is "YES" if and only if there exists at least one group that does not contain both a Rick and a Morty from the same universe.

- Group 1: only Rick from universe 3 → this works (we can make Rick from universe 3 the traitor)
- Answer is YES → correct

- Group 1: members from universes 3, -2, 1, -1, 5 → this means Morty from 3, Rick from 2, Morty from 1, Rick from 1, Morty from 5
- This has: Rick from 1 and Morty from 1 → conflict in universe 1 → cannot have both as traitors

- Actually, group 2 has Morty from 5 and Rick from 2 → no conflict between same universe
- But do we have both Rick and Morty from same universe in any group?

So we have Rick from universe 1 and Morty from universe 1 → conflict!

- -5 → Rick from 5

Example 3:
- Group 1: -1, 6, 7 → Rick from 1, Morty from 6, Morty from 7
- So from universe 1: only Rick, so no conflict with any Morty from 1
- From universe 6: only Morty
- From universe 7: only Morty
- No conflict → this group can have all traitors!

Answer is YES → correct.

## Solution Algorithm

For each group, check if it contains both a Rick and a Morty from the same universe.

If any group does not contain any conflict (no universe has both Rick and Morty members), then answer is "YES".

Steps:
1. For each group, collect the universes and their types (Rick or Morty)
2. For each universe, check if both Rick and Morty appear in the group
3. If no such conflict exists in a group, return "YES"
4. If no group has no conflict, return "NO"

Wait, actually if a group has no conflict (no universe with both Rick and Morty), then we can assign traitors such that all members are traitors (just make the member from that universe the traitor).

So we just need to check if there's any group with no conflicting members.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

def has_conflict(group):
    # group contains list of members (positive = Morty, negative = Rick)
    # extract universe and type for each member
    universe_rick = set()
    universe_morty = set()
    
    for member in group:
        if member > 0:
            universe_morty.add(member)
        else:
            universe_rick.add(-member)
    
    # Check if any universe has both Rick and Morty
    for uni in universe_rick:
        if uni in universe_morty:
            return True
    
    return False

# Check each group
found_no_conflict = False
for _ in range(m):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    k = line[0]
    members = line[1:k+1]
    
    # Check if this group has conflict
    if not has_conflict(members):
        found_no_conflict = True
        break

if found_no_conflict:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

def has_conflict(group):

    # extract universe and type for each member

    universe_morty = set()

        else:
            universe_rick.add(-member)
    
    # Check if any universe has both Rick and Morty

        if uni in universe_morty:
            return True
    
    return False

# Check each group
found_no_conflict = False
for _ in range(m):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    k = line[0]
    members = line[1:k+1]
    
    # Check if this group has conflict
    if not has_conflict(members):
        found_no_conflict = True
        break

if found_no_conflict:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` parallel universes, each with one Rick and one Morty
2. In each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor (the other is loyal)
3. There are `m` groups, and each person can join multiple groups
4. We need to determine if there exists ANY scenario where a group has ALL traitors (the world ends)
5. If such a scenario exists, output "YES", otherwise "NO"

## Key Insight

For a group to have all traitors, every member in that group must be a traitor in their respective universe.

For a group to have all traitors, we need to check if there's a way to assign traitors such that:
- For each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor
- All members of a particular group are traitors

The question is: does there exist a group where we can make all its members traitors while satisfying the constraint that in each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor?

## Analysis Approach

For each group, we need to check if it's possible for all members of that group to be traitors.

For a group to have all traitors:
1. For each person in the group, they must be a traitor
2. This means for each universe that appears in the group:
   - If it's Rick from universe `u`, then Rick is a traitor (so Morty is loyal)
   - If it's Morty from universe `u`, then Morty is a traitor (so Rick is loyal)

The key constraint is that in each universe, exactly one person is a traitor.

So for a group, we need to check if there's a conflict in the universes it references.

For each universe that appears in the group, we get a constraint:
- If Rick appears in the group, Rick must be a traitor
- If Morty appears in the group, Morty must be a traitor

The constraint is that in each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor. So if both Rick and Morty from the same universe appear in the group, that's impossible (both can't be traitors).

## Algorithm

1. For each group, check if any universe has both its Rick and Morty appearing in that group
2. If such a conflict exists in any group, then there's a scenario where that group has all traitors → answer is "YES"
3. If no group has such a conflict, answer is "NO"

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, we don't need to check for conflicts in the same group. For a group to have all traitors, we just need to ensure that for each universe, we can assign exactly one traitor.

For each universe in a group:
- If the group contains only Rick from universe `u`, then Rick must be traitor
- If the group contains only Morty from universe `u`, then Morty must be traitor  
- If the group contains both Rick and Morty from universe `u`, then it's impossible for both to be traitors

Therefore, for a group to have all traitors, no universe should appear with both Rick and Morty in that group.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. For each group, go through each member
2. Keep track of which universes have Rick or Morty appearing
3. For each universe, check if both Rick and Morty appear in the same group
4. If any group has both Rick and Morty from the same universe, then we can assign traitors to make that group have all traitors → answer "YES"
5. If no such group exists, answer "NO"

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

should_cancel = False

for _ in range(m):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    k = line[0]
    members = line[1:]
    
    # Dictionary to track which universes have Rick or Morty in this group
    universe_roles = {}  # universe -> role (None, Rick, Morty)
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            # Rick from universe -member
            universe = -member
            role = "Rick"
        else:
            # Morty from universe member
            universe = member
            role = "Morty"
        
        if universe in universe_roles:
            # Conflict: already have role for this universe
            if universe_roles[universe] != role:
                should_cancel = True
                break
        else:
            universe_roles[universe] = role
    
    # If we found a conflict in this group, we can cancel the event
    if should_cancel:
        break

print("YES" if should_cancel else "NO")
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Since the giant heads have appeared in the sky all humanity is in danger, so all Ricks and Mortys from all parallel universes are gathering in groups to find a solution to get rid of them. 

There are n parallel universes participating in this event (n Ricks and n Mortys). I. e. each of n universes has one Rick and one Morty. They're gathering in m groups. Each person can be in many groups and a group can contain an arbitrary number of members.

Ricks and Mortys have registered online in these groups. So, a person can have joined a group more than once (developer of this website hadn't considered this possibility). [Image] 

Summer from universe #1 knows that in each parallel universe (including hers) exactly one of Rick and Morty from that universe is a traitor and is loyal, but no one knows which one. She knows that we are doomed if there's a group such that every member in that group is a traitor (they will plan and destroy the world). 

Summer knows that if there's a possibility that world ends (there's a group where all members are traitors) she should immediately cancel this event. So she wants to know if she should cancel the event. You have to tell her yes if and only if there's at least one scenario (among all 2^{n} possible scenarios, 2 possible scenarios for who a traitor in each universe) such that in that scenario the world will end.


-----Input-----

The first line of input contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^4) — number of universes and number of groups respectively.

The next m lines contain the information about the groups. i-th of them first contains an integer k (number of times someone joined i-th group, k > 0) followed by k integers v_{i}, 1, v_{i}, 2, ..., v_{i}, k. If v_{i}, j is negative, it means that Rick from universe number  - v_{i}, j has joined this group and otherwise it means that Morty from universe number v_{i}, j has joined it.

Sum of k for all groups does not exceed 10^4.


-----Output-----

In a single line print the answer to Summer's question. Print "YES" if she should cancel the event and "NO" otherwise.


-----Examples-----
Input
4 2
1 -3
4 -2 3 2 -3

Output
YES

Input
5 2
5 3 -2 1 -1 5
3 -5 2 5

Output
NO

Input
7 2
3 -1 6 7
7 -5 4 2 4 7 -3 4

Output
YES

In the first sample testcase, 1st group only contains the Rick from universe number 3, so in case he's a traitor, then all members of this group are traitors and so Summer should cancel the event.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` universes, each with one Rick and one Morty
2. In each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor (not both, not neither)
3. We have `m` groups, each containing members from various universes
4. We need to determine if there exists ANY scenario where a group has ALL members as traitors
5. If such a scenario exists, we output "YES", otherwise "NO"

## Key Insight

For a group to have all members as traitors, we need to check if there's a way to assign traitors such that:
- In each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor
- For a specific group, ALL members in that group are traitors

The key insight is that we can check each group independently to see if it's possible for ALL members in that group to be traitors, while satisfying the constraint that in each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor.

## Analysis for a Single Group

For a group to have all members as traitors:
- For each universe represented in the group, we need to ensure that the member in that universe is the traitor
- If a universe has both Rick and Morty in the group, this is impossible (we can't have both as traitors since exactly one is a traitor per universe)
- If a universe has only one member (Rick or Morty), we can make that member the traitor

However, we need to be careful about the constraint: in each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor.

## Algorithm

For each group:
1. Identify all universes that have members in this group
2. For each universe, check if the members in the group are valid
3. If there's a universe that has both Rick and Morty in the group, this group cannot have all traitors
4. If there's a universe that has only one member (either Rick or Morty), we can make that member the traitor
5. If for every universe in the group, we can assign exactly one traitor (specifically, the member in the group), then this group can have all traitors

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, for a group to have all traitors:
- For each universe, we need to choose exactly one of Rick or Morty to be the traitor
- For the group to have all traitors, every member in the group must be the traitor from their universe

So for a group to be possible:
- In each universe represented in the group, we must have either:
  - Only Rick in the group → we can make Rick the traitor
  - Only Morty in the group → we can make Morty the traitor
  - Both Rick and Morty in the group → impossible (can't have both as traitors)

Actually, let me think again. If both Rick and Morty are in the same group, then we would need both to be traitors, which violates the "exactly one traitor per universe" rule.

Therefore, for a group to be possible where all members are traitors:
- No universe should have both Rick and Morty in the group
- For each universe in the group, we can assign the traitor to be the specific member who is in the group

So a group can have all traitors if and only if no universe appears with both its Rick and Morty in the group.

Wait, that's not quite right either. Let me reframe:

For a group to have all members as traitors:
- We need to assign exactly one traitor per universe
- For each universe represented in the group, we need to assign the specific member (Rick or Morty) who is in the group to be the traitor

The constraint is that for each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor.

So for a group to be possible to have all traitors:
- For each universe, if both Rick and Morty are in the group, then we would need both to be traitors, which is impossible
- If only one of Rick or Morty is in the group, we can make that one the traitor

Therefore, a group can have all traitors if and only if for every universe that appears in the group, exactly one of Rick or Morty appears in the group.

So we need to check for each group whether there exists a universe that has both Rick and Morty in it.

## Algorithm

For each group:
1. Create a set of universes and check for each universe whether both Rick and Morty are present
2. If we find any universe that has both Rick and Morty in the group, then this group cannot have all traitors
3. If we don't find such a universe, then this group CAN have all traitors (by making the member in the group the traitor)

If ANY group can have all traitors, we output "YES", otherwise "NO".

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Check if any group can have all traitors
can_end_world = False

for _ in range(m):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    k = line[0]
    members = line[1:]
    
    # Track which universes have Rick and Morty in this group
    universe_rick = set()
    universe_morty = set()
    
    for member in members:
        if member < 0:
            # Negative means Rick from universe -member
            universe_rick.add(-member)
        else:
            # Positive means Morty from universe member
            universe_morty.add(member)
    
    # Check if any universe has both Rick and Morty in this group
    has_both = False
    for universe in universe_rick:
        if universe in universe_morty:
            has_both = True
            break
    
    # If no universe has both Rick and Morty, then this group can have all traitors
    if not has_both:
        can_end_world = True
        break

if can_end_world:
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` parallel universes, each with one Rick and one Morty
2. In each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor (so for each universe, we have 2 possibilities)
3. We have `m` groups, each containing members from various universes
4. We need to determine if there exists ANY scenario where at least one group has ALL members as traitors
5. If such a scenario exists, Summer should cancel the event (output "YES"), otherwise "NO"

## Key Insight

For a group to have all traitors, every member in that group must be a traitor in their respective universe.

Since in each universe exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor, we can think of it as:
- For each universe, we choose whether Rick is the traitor or Morty is the traitor
- We want to know if there's a combination of choices such that for at least one group, all members are traitors

## Strategy

Instead of checking all 2^n scenarios (which is too expensive), we can use a different approach:

For each group, we can ask: "Can we make all members of this group traitors?"

For a group to have all traitors:
- For each member in the group:
  - If it's Rick from universe `u`, then Rick must be the traitor in universe `u`
  - If it's Morty from universe `u`, then Morty must be the traitor in universe `u`

The question is whether we can assign traitors to each universe such that for at least one group, all members are traitors.

Notice that for a group to have all traitors, the traitor assignments for each universe in that group must be consistent. Specifically:
- For Rick in universe `u`: Rick is traitor
- For Morty in universe `u`: Morty is traitor

If we have a group where we have both Rick from universe `u` and Morty from universe `u`, this is impossible because exactly one of them is a traitor in each universe.

So for a group to have all traitors, we can only have members from the same universe if they are both the same type (either both are Ricks from different universes, or both are Mortys from different universes).

Actually, let me reconsider: we can have members from different universes, but for each universe, we need to assign either Rick or Morty as traitor.

1. For each universe that appears in the group, we assign the traitor to be the member that appears in that universe
2. This is only possible if for each universe in the group, we don't have both Rick and Morty assigned

Wait, I think I'm overcomplicating. Let me think differently.

For a group to have all traitors, we need to assign traitors to each universe such that:
- For each member in the group, the person specified is the traitor in their universe
- This assignment is only possible if for each universe, we don't have conflicting assignments (both Rick and Morty from the same universe in the same group)

But actually, for each universe, we only have one Rick and one Morty, so if a universe appears with both Rick and Morty in the same group, that's impossible.

So for a group to have all traitors, we need that no universe appears in the group with both Rick and Morty.

More precisely: if a universe appears in a group with both Rick and Morty, then we cannot have all members as traitors in that group.

Therefore, for each group, we can check if it's possible to have all members as traitors. This is possible if and only if for every universe that appears in the group, it appears with at most one type of person (either only Rick or only Morty).

Example 2:

## Solution

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    for _ in range(m):
        line = list(map(int, input().split()))
        k = line[0]
        members = line[1:k+1]
        
        # Track which universes have both Rick and Morty in this group
        universe_conflict = set()
        
        for member in members:
            if member < 0:
                # Rick from -member
                universe = -member
            else:
                # Morty from member
                universe = member
            
            # Check if this universe already has a conflict
            # We need to know if this universe appears with both types
            # Since we process each member, we can just track if we've seen both
            # Actually, we need to know if the same universe appears with both Rick and Morty
            
        # Instead, let's process and check conflicts
        universe_types = {}  # universe -> set of types (rick or morty)
        
        for member in members:
            if member < 0:
                universe = -member
                type_ = "rick"
            else:
                universe = member
                type_ = "morty"
            
            if universe in universe_types:
                # Already have a type for this universe
                if universe_types[universe] != type_:
                    # Conflict: we have both rick and morty from same universe
                    universe_types[universe] = "both"
                    # We found a conflict, this group can't have all traitors
                    break
            else:
                universe_types[universe] = type_
        else:
            # Didn't break, so no conflicts found in this group
            # This group could potentially have all traitors
            print("YES")
            return
        
    # If we get here, no group had no conflicts
    print("NO")

solve()
```

The next m lines contain the information about the groups. i-th of them first contains an integer k (number of times someone joined i-th group, k > 0) followed by k integers v_{i}, 1, v_{i}, 2, ..., v_{i}, k. If v_{i}, j is negative, it means that Rick from universe number  - v_{i}, j has joined this group and otherwise it means that Morty from universe number v_{i}, j has joined it.

Sum of k for all groups does not exceed 10^4.


-----Output-----

In a single line print the answer to Summer's question. Print "YES" if she should cancel the event and "NO" otherwise.


-----Examples-----
Input
4 2
1 -3
4 -2 3 2 -3

Output
YES

Input
5 2
5 3 -2 1 -1 5
3 -5 2 5

Output
NO

Input
7 2
3 -1 6 7
7 -5 4 2 4 7 -3 4

Output
YES



-----Note-----

In the first sample testcase, 1st group only contains the Rick from universe number 3, so in case he's a traitor, then all members of this group are traitors and so Summer should cancel the event.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. There are `n` parallel universes, each with one Rick and one Morty
2. In each universe, exactly one of Rick or Morty is a traitor (the other is loyal)
3. We have `m` groups, where each person (Rick or Morty) can join multiple groups
4. A group is dangerous if ALL members in that group are traitors
5. We need to determine if there exists ANY scenario where a group has all traitors

## Key Insight

For a group to be dangerous (all members are traitors), we need to find a scenario where:
- For each member in the group, that specific person is the traitor
- Since each universe has exactly one traitor (either Rick or Morty), we can't have both be traitors

For a group to be dangerous, we need to check if there's a way to assign traitors such that all members of the group are traitors.

## Analysis

For a group to be dangerous, we need to assign traitors to members such that:
- Each universe has exactly one traitor
- All members in the group are traitors

This means that for any universe that appears as a member in the group, we must choose the specific person (Rick or Morty) as the traitor.

The key insight is that if a universe appears with both Rick and Morty in the same group, then we cannot have both be traitors (since each universe has exactly one traitor). Therefore, we can only have a dangerous group if for every universe that appears in the group, we can assign exactly one person as the traitor.

However, there's a more fundamental constraint: if a group contains both Rick from universe `i` and Morty from universe `i`, then we cannot have both be traitors. So such a group cannot be dangerous.

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, the problem is simpler:

For a group to be dangerous, we need to assign traitors such that all members of the group are traitors. This is only possible if we can assign traitors consistently across all universes.

For a specific group to be dangerous, we need to check if there's a consistent assignment where all members of the group are traitors.

The crucial point: if a universe appears in the group with both Rick and Morty, then we cannot make both of them traitors (since each universe has exactly one traitor). So if a group contains both Rick from universe i and Morty from universe i, then it's impossible for all members to be traitors.

Therefore, for a group to be dangerous, no universe can appear with both its Rick and Morty in that group.

But we don't need to check every group - we just need to know if there exists ANY scenario where a group has all traitors.

So we should check if there exists at least one group where no universe has both its Rick and Morty as members.

Wait, that's not right either. Let me think again.

Actually, for a group to have all traitors, we need to assign traitors such that all members are traitors. For each universe, we choose one of Rick or Morty as traitor.

For a specific group, we need to check if it's possible to assign traitors to make all members of the group traitors.

For a universe that appears in the group with both Rick and Morty, it's impossible (since only one can be traitor).

So a group can be dangerous only if for every universe that appears in it, only one of Rick or Morty appears.

Therefore, we just need to check if there exists at least one group such that no universe appears with both its Rick and Morty.

If such a group exists, then we can assign the traitor to be the member who appears in that group, and we can always satisfy the constraint that each universe has exactly one traitor.

Actually, I think I'm overcomplicating. The simpler answer is:

For a group to be dangerous, we need to find an assignment of traitors such that all members in the group are traitors. 

This is impossible if any universe has both Rick and Morty in the group. 

Therefore, we need to check if there exists at least one group where for every universe, only one of Rick or Morty appears.

If such a group exists, then it's possible for that group to have all traitors, so answer is "YES".

If all groups have at least one universe where both Rick and Morty appear, then no group can have all traitors, so answer is "NO".

But wait - what if a group has only one person from a universe? That's fine.

So the algorithm is:

1. For each group, check if it contains both Rick and Morty from the same universe
2. If any group has no such conflicts, then we can have a scenario where all members of that group are traitors
3. Return "YES" if such a group exists, "NO" otherwise

## Step-by-step solution

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

def has_conflict(group):
    # group is a list of entries, where negative means Rick, positive means Morty
    # track which universes have both Rick and Morty
    universe_has_rick = set()
    universe_has_morty = set()
    
    for member in group:
        if member < 0:
            # Rick from universe -member
            universe = -member
            universe_has_rick.add(universe)
        else:
            # Morty from universe member
            universe = member
            universe_has_morty.add(universe)
    
    # Check if any universe has both Rick and Morty
    for universe in universe_has_rick:
        if universe in universe_has_morty:
            return True
    return False

# Check each group
for _ in range(m):
    line = list(map(int, input().split()))
    k = line[0]
    group = line[1:k+1]
    
    # If this group has no conflict (no universe has both Rick and Morty), then it can be dangerous
    if not has_conflict(group):
        print("YES")
        exit()

# If no group has conflict, then it's impossible for any group to have all traitors
print("NO")
```

- 6: Morty from 6

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Anadi has a set of dominoes. Every domino has two parts, and each part contains some dots. For every a and b such that 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6, there is exactly one domino with a dots on one half and b dots on the other half. The set contains exactly 21 dominoes. Here is an exact illustration of his set:

<image>

Also, Anadi has an undirected graph without self-loops and multiple edges. He wants to choose some dominoes and place them on the edges of this graph. He can use at most one domino of each type. Each edge can fit at most one domino. It's not necessary to place a domino on each edge of the graph.

When placing a domino on an edge, he also chooses its direction. In other words, one half of any placed domino must be directed toward one of the endpoints of the edge and the other half must be directed toward the other endpoint. There's a catch: if there are multiple halves of dominoes directed toward the same vertex, each of these halves must contain the same number of dots.

How many dominoes at most can Anadi place on the edges of his graph?

Input

The first line contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n ≤ 7, 0 ≤ m ≤ (n⋅(n-1))/(2)) — the number of vertices and the number of edges in the graph.

The next m lines contain two integers each. Integers in the i-th line are a_i and b_i (1 ≤ a, b ≤ n, a ≠ b) and denote that there is an edge which connects vertices a_i and b_i.

The graph might be disconnected. It's however guaranteed that the graph doesn't contain any self-loops, and that there is at most one edge between any pair of vertices.

Output

Output one integer which denotes the maximum number of dominoes which Anadi can place on the edges of the graph.

Examples

Input


4 4
1 2
2 3
3 4
4 1


Output


4


Input


7 0


Output


0

3 1
1 3

1

6 7

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have 21 dominoes with values (a,b) where 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6
2. We have an undirected graph with n vertices and m edges
3. We want to place dominoes on edges such that:
   - Each edge gets at most one domino
   - When placing a domino on an edge between vertices u and v, we assign direction (u→v or v→u)
   - For each vertex, all domino halves directed toward it must have the same number of dots
4. We need to maximize the number of dominoes placed

## Key Insights

1. The constraint about "all halves directed toward the same vertex must have the same number of dots" is crucial.
2. This means that if we assign a value to vertex v, all incoming domino halves to v must have that same value.
3. For each edge (u,v), if we place a domino with value (a,b), then:
   - If we orient it u→v, then vertex u gets a dots, vertex v gets b dots
   - If we orient it v→u, then vertex v gets a dots, vertex u gets b dots
4. We need to assign a value to each vertex such that for every edge, the values assigned to the endpoints match the domino values.

## Solution Approach

Since n ≤ 7, we can use state space search or dynamic programming with bitmasks. However, a more direct approach is to use backtracking with pruning.

Actually, let me think of this differently. We need to:
1. For each possible assignment of values to vertices (each vertex gets a value from 1 to 6), check if we can place dominoes on all edges such that the constraints are satisfied.
2. The total number of dominoes placed is the number of edges for which there exists a domino (a,b) such that one endpoint has value a and the other has value b, and a ≤ b (to avoid double counting).

Wait, no - we can orient dominoes arbitrarily. For each edge (u,v), we can use any domino (a,b) with a ≤ b, and orient it so that either u has a and v has b, or u has b and v has a. But the constraint is that all domino halves going to a vertex must have the same value.

So for a valid assignment of values to vertices, for each edge (u,v), if the values assigned to u and v are x and y, we can use the domino (min(x,y), max(x,y)) because:
- We can orient it appropriately
- The constraint is satisfied since all halves to vertex u have value x, and all halves to vertex v have value y.

Therefore, the maximum number of dominoes we can place is the number of edges where the values assigned to the endpoints form a valid domino (i.e., the values are from 1 to 6, and we can use domino (min(x,y), max(x,y)).

But we need to ensure that the domino (min(x,y), max(x,y)) exists, which it does for any values from 1 to 6 since 1 ≤ min(x,y) ≤ max(x,y) ≤ 6.

So for any assignment of values 1-6 to vertices, we can place a domino on an edge if and only if both endpoints have values in 1-6 (which they always do) and we use domino (min(x,y), max(x,y)).

However, we can only use each domino type once. So we need to ensure that we don't use the same domino type more than once across different edges.

This is getting complex. Let me reconsider.

Actually, since we have exactly one domino of each type (a,b) with 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6, and we can use at most one domino per type, we need to assign values to vertices such that:
- For each edge, we assign values to its endpoints that form a domino (a,b)
- No domino type is used more than once

Since n ≤ 7, we can try all possible assignments of values 1-6 to the n vertices (but only n vertices, not all 6 values needed).

Actually, each vertex can have any value from 1 to 6, so there are 6^n possible assignments. Since n ≤ 7, 6^7 = 279,936, which is manageable.

For each assignment:
1. For each edge (u,v), let x = value[u], y = value[v]
2. The domino needed is (min(x,y), max(x,y))
3. Count how many times each domino type appears across all edges
4. The total valid dominoes we can place is the sum over edges of 1, but we need to ensure no domino type is used more than once.

Actually, if a domino type appears k times, we can only use it once, so we can place at most 1 domino per distinct domino type.

But we need to place dominoes on edges. For each edge, we need a domino (a,b). If the same domino (a,b) is needed for multiple edges, we can only use it once.

So for a given assignment, the number of dominoes we can place is the number of distinct domino types that are needed across edges.

So the maximum number of dominoes we can place with this assignment is the number of distinct domino types among the edges.

But we want to maximize the total number of dominoes, so we should consider that we might not place dominoes on all edges in the assignment.

Actually, we can place a domino on an edge if the domino (min(x,y), max(x,y)) is available (not used by any other edge).

For a given assignment, the number of dominoes we can place is the number of edges such that the domino (min(x,y), max(x,y)) has not been used by any other edge.

We can calculate this as: for each domino type, we can use it at most once. So if k edges require the same domino type, we can only use it for one edge.

Therefore, for a given assignment, the maximum number of dominoes we can place is the number of distinct domino types needed.

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: n=4, m=4, cycle of 4 vertices. Output is 4.

Example 2: n=7, m=0. Output is 0.

Example 3: n=3, m=1, edge (1,3). Output is 1.

Example 4: complete graph K7, m=21, output is 16.

I see that in example 4, with 21 edges, we get only 16 dominoes, which suggests that many domino types are duplicated.

So the algorithm is:

1. Try all possible assignments of values (from 1 to 6) to the n vertices
2. For each assignment, for each edge (u,v), compute the required domino (min(val[u],val[v]), max(val[u],val[v]))
3. Count the frequency of each domino type
4. The number of distinct domino types is the number of edges we can place dominoes on (since each domino type can be used only once)
5. Take the maximum over all assignments

But wait, is it really the number of distinct domino types? Yes, because each domino type can only be used once.

```python
from itertools import product

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    edges = []
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        edges.append((u-1, v-1))  # Convert to 0-indexed
    
    if m == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    # Try all possible assignments of values 1-6 to n vertices
    # Each vertex gets a value from 1 to 6
    for values in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
        # For this assignment, compute which domino types are needed
        domino_count = {}
        
        # For each edge, compute the required domino
        for u, v in edges:
            val_u, val_v = values[u], values[v]
            min_val, max_val = min(val_u, val_v), max(val_u, val_v)
            domino = (min_val, max_val)
            
            domino_count[domino] = domino_count.get(domino, 0) + 1
        
        # The number of distinct domino types is the maximum number of dominoes we can place
        # (each domino type can be used at most once)
        distinct_dominoes = len(domino_count)
        max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, distinct_dominoes)
    
    print(max_dominoes)

solve()
```

Wait, this doesn't seem right. For a cycle of 4 vertices, can we really get 4 dominoes? Let me check.

In a cycle of 4 vertices, if we assign values so that each edge has a different domino, we could get 4 distinct dominoes. That seems possible.

But consider example 4: complete graph K7 with 21 edges, output is 16. That means in the best assignment, we only get 16 distinct domino types, which makes sense because there are many duplicate domino types.

However, I'm concerned about the logic. The number of distinct domino types needed for the edges is indeed the maximum number of dominoes we can place (since we can only use each type once).

But let me verify with a smaller example. For 3 vertices with edge (1,3), we can assign values to vertices. The only edge requires one domino type, so we get 1 domino. That matches.

So the algorithm seems correct.

Let me run through the complete graph example mentally. In K7, we have many edges. With many vertices, it's likely that many edges will require the same domino type (e.g., all edges between vertices with values 1,2 will require domino (1,2)). The best assignment will minimize duplicate domino types, but we're maximizing distinct types.

Since the answer is 16, and there are 21 edges, we're using 16 different domino types, meaning 5 domino types are used twice or more - this makes sense.

```python
from itertools import product

n, m = map(int, input().split())

edges = []
for _ in range(m):
    u, v = map(int, input().split())
    edges.append((u-1, v-1))  # Convert to 0-indexed

if m == 0:
    print(0)
else:
    max_dominoes = 0

        # For this assignment, compute which domino types are needed
        domino_count = {}
        
        # For each edge, compute the required domino
        for u, v in edges:
            val_u, val_v = values[u], values[v]
            min_val, max_val = min(val_u, val_v), max(val_u, val_v)
            domino = (min_val, max_val)
            
            domino_count[domino] = domino_count.get(domino, 0) + 1
```

```python
from itertools import product

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    edges = []
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        # Convert to 0-indexed
        edges.append((u-1, v-1))
    
    if m == 0:
        return 0
    
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    # Try all possible value assignments to vertices (each vertex gets value 1-6)
    # There are 6^n assignments
    for values in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
        # For this assignment, what are the distinct domino pairs we can use?
        domino_pairs = set()
        
        for u, v in edges:
            val_u, val_v = values[u], values[v]
            # The domino is (min, max) to avoid duplicates
            domino_pair = (min(val_u, val_v), max(val_u, val_v))
            domino_pairs.add(domino_pair)
        
        max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, len(domino_pairs))
    
    return max_dominoes

print(solve())
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Anadi has a set of dominoes. Every domino has two parts, and each part contains some dots. For every a and b such that 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6, there is exactly one domino with a dots on one half and b dots on the other half. The set contains exactly 21 dominoes. Here is an exact illustration of his set:

<image>

Also, Anadi has an undirected graph without self-loops and multiple edges. He wants to choose some dominoes and place them on the edges of this graph. He can use at most one domino of each type. Each edge can fit at most one domino. It's not necessary to place a domino on each edge of the graph.

When placing a domino on an edge, he also chooses its direction. In other words, one half of any placed domino must be directed toward one of the endpoints of the edge and the other half must be directed toward the other endpoint. There's a catch: if there are multiple halves of dominoes directed toward the same vertex, each of these halves must contain the same number of dots.

How many dominoes at most can Anadi place on the edges of his graph?

Input

The first line contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n ≤ 7, 0 ≤ m ≤ (n⋅(n-1))/(2)) — the number of vertices and the number of edges in the graph.

The next m lines contain two integers each. Integers in the i-th line are a_i and b_i (1 ≤ a, b ≤ n, a ≠ b) and denote that there is an edge which connects vertices a_i and b_i.

The graph might be disconnected. It's however guaranteed that the graph doesn't contain any self-loops, and that there is at most one edge between any pair of vertices.

Output

Output one integer which denotes the maximum number of dominoes which Anadi can place on the edges of the graph.

Examples

Input


4 4
1 2
2 3
3 4
4 1


Output


4


Input


7 0

0

1

Since we can only use each domino type once, the maximum we can place is the number of distinct domino types that appear in the edge set.

Wait - actually, for a fixed value assignment, if we process all edges, we get a set of domino types. We can place exactly one domino for each distinct type that appears. So the count is just the number of distinct domino types among all edges (the value assignment is fixed).

But we might be able to choose a subset of edges to maximize the distinct domino types? Actually, no - since we're given the edges, and for each edge, we can only use one domino type, and if we don't use an edge, we can't get that domino type.

But actually, we can only place a domino on an edge if the domino type is available. Since we have all domino types, we can place a domino on any edge that we want, as long as the domino type we need is not already used.

For a given value assignment, we can find the maximum number of dominoes we can place by greedily selecting edges with distinct domino types (we could just take one edge per distinct type).

So for each value assignment, the number of dominoes we can place is equal to the number of distinct domino types that appear in the edges (since we can always place one domino per distinct type, and we can't place more than one per type).

Therefore, the algorithm is:

1. Try all possible assignments of values 1-6 to each of the n vertices (6^n assignments)
2. For each assignment:
   - For each edge (u,v), compute domino type (min(value[u], value[v]), max(value[u], value[v]))
   - Count the number of distinct domino types among the edges
3. Return the maximum count across all assignments

But we don't need to actually try all edges - we just need to consider only the existing edges in the graph.

The maximum number of dominoes we can place is the maximum over all assignments of the number of distinct domino types among the edges.

## Optimization

We can optimize by noting that we only care about the distinct types among the edges. We don't need to consider all 6^n assignments - we can use dynamic programming or pruning, but given that n ≤ 7 and 6^7 = 279,936, which is acceptable.

Actually, 6^7 = 279,936, which is manageable.

```python
from itertools import product

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    edges = []
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        # Convert to 0-indexed
        edges.append((u-1, v-1))
    
    if m == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    # Try all possible value assignments to vertices (each vertex gets value 1-6)
    for values in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
        # For this assignment, find distinct domino types among edges
        domino_types = set()
        for u, v in edges:
            a, b = values[u], values[v]
            # Sort to get canonical form (a, b) where a <= b
            if a > b:
                a, b = b, a
            domino_types.add((a, b))
        
        # The number of distinct domino types is the maximum dominoes we can place
        num_dominoes = len(domino_types)
        max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, num_dominoes)
    
    print(max_dominoes)

solve()
```

How many dominoes at most can Anadi place on the edges of his graph?

Output

We can choose to place a domino on an edge only if that domino type hasn't been used yet. 1. Try all possible assignments of values from 1 to 6 to the n vertices
2. For each assignment, compute the set of domino types that appear on edges

## Implementation

```python
from itertools import product

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    edges = []
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        edges.append((u-1, v-1))  # convert to 0-indexed
    
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    # Try all possible assignments of values (1-6) to vertices
    for values in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
        # For this assignment, find all domino types used on edges
        domino_types = set()
        
        for u, v in edges:
            val_u, val_v = values[u], values[v]
            # Create domino type with smaller value first
            a, b = min(val_u, val_v), max(val_u, val_v)
            domino_types.add((a, b))
        
        # Number of distinct domino types is the number of dominoes we can place
        max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, len(domino_types))
    
    print(max_dominoes)

solve()
```

- If we assign values (1,2,3,4), edges give types (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (1,4) - 4 distinct types.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a set of 21 dominoes where each domino has two parts with dots (a,b) where 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6
2. We have an undirected graph with n vertices and m edges
3. We want to place dominoes on edges such that:
   - Each edge gets at most one domino
   - When placing a domino on an edge (u,v), we assign one half to u and one half to v
   - For each vertex, all domino halves directed toward it must have the same number of dots
   - We want to maximize the number of dominoes placed

## Key Insight

The constraint about vertices having consistent dot values is crucial. If we place dominoes on edges incident to a vertex, all the domino halves facing that vertex must have the same number of dots.

This means we can think of this as assigning a "value" to each vertex, and then:
- For each edge (u,v), if we place a domino, we must assign values x and y such that x = value[u] and y = value[v]
- But since we're constrained by the domino set (we can only use dominoes where 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6), we need x and y to form a valid domino pair (x,y) where x ≤ y

Actually, let me reconsider. For a domino placed on edge (u,v), we can assign it so that one half goes to u with value a, and the other half goes to v with value b. The constraint is that all domino halves facing the same vertex must have the same value.

This means that for each vertex, we assign a single value (say v[u]), and every domino placed on edges connected to u must have one half equal to v[u].

So for edge (u,v), if we place a domino, we must have one half with value v[u] going to u, and one half with value v[v] going to v. This means we need a domino with values (v[u], v[v]) where v[u] ≤ v[v].

## Algorithm Approach

Since n ≤ 7, we can try all possible assignments of values to vertices. Each vertex can have a value from 1 to 6 (since domino values are 1-6).

For each possible assignment of values to vertices (v[1], v[2], ..., v[n]), we can:
1. For each edge (u,v), check if we can place a domino on it
2. We can place a domino on edge (u,v) if and only if (v[u], v[v]) forms a valid domino, i.e., v[u] ≤ v[v]
3. The total number of dominoes we can place is the sum over all edges of 1 if we can place a domino, 0 otherwise

We need to find the assignment that maximizes this count.

Since n ≤ 7, there are at most 7 vertices, and each can have values from 1 to 6, so total possibilities are 6^7 = 279,936, which is manageable.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input
2. Generate all possible value assignments for vertices (value[1] to value[n])
3. For each assignment:
   - Count how many edges can have a domino placed (the edge (u,v) can have a domino if value[u] ≤ value[v])
4. Return the maximum count across all assignments

```python
from itertools import product

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())

edges = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    edges.append((a, b))

# Try all possible value assignments for vertices 1 to n
# Each vertex can have value from 1 to 6
max_dominoes = 0

# Generate all combinations of values for n vertices
for values in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
    # Convert to 0-indexed for easier handling
    vertex_values = [0] + list(values)  # vertex_values[i] is value at vertex i
    
    # Count dominoes we can place on edges
    dominoes_count = 0
    for u, v in edges:
        # u and v are 1-indexed, convert to 0-indexed
        # Check if we can place a domino on edge (u,v)
        # We need a domino with values (vertex_values[u], vertex_values[v])
        # Since dominoes have a ≤ b, we need vertex_values[u] <= vertex_values[v]
        if vertex_values[u] <= vertex_values[v]:
            dominoes_count += 1
    
    max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, dominoes_count)

print(max_dominoes)
```

```python
from itertools import product

# Read input
n, m = map(int, input().split())

edges = []
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    edges.append((a, b))

# Try all possible value assignments for vertices 1 to n
# Each vertex can have value from 1 to 6
max_dominoes = 0

# Generate all combinations of values for n vertices
for values in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
    # Convert to 0-indexed for easier handling
    # vertex_values[i] is value at vertex i+1
    vertex_values = [0] + list(values)
    
    # Count dominoes we can place on edges
    dominoes_count = 0
    for u, v in edges:
        # u and v are 1-indexed, so vertex_values[u] and vertex_values[v] are values
        if vertex_values[u] <= vertex_values[v]:
            dominoes_count += 1
    
    max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, dominoes_count)

print(max_dominoes)
```

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    edges = []
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        edges.append((u-1, v-1))  # Convert to 0-indexed
    
    # If no edges, answer is 0
    if m == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Find vertices that have incident edges
    incident_vertices = set()
    for u, v in edges:
        incident_vertices.add(u)
        incident_vertices.add(v)
    
    # If no vertex has incident edges, answer is 0
    if not incident_vertices:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # We need to assign values 1-6 to each vertex in incident_vertices
    # For each assignment, count how many edges can have dominoes
    
    # Convert to list of vertices
    vertex_list = list(incident_vertices)
    
    # Try all possible assignments
    max_dominoes = 0
    
    # Use recursion to generate all assignments
    def backtrack(index, assignment):
        nonlocal max_dominoes
        
        if index == len(vertex_list):
            # Count dominoes that can be placed
            count = 0
            for u, v in edges:
                val_u = assignment[u]
                val_v = assignment[v]
                # Since we have domino (min, max) for any pair, we can always place
                # Actually, we just need to check if the domino exists - it does
                count += 1
            max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, count)
            return
        
        # Assign value 1 to 6 to current vertex
        for val in range(1, 7):
            assignment[vertex_list[index]] = val
            backtrack(index + 1, assignment)
    
    # Initialize assignment dictionary
    assignment = {}
    backtrack(0, assignment)
    
    print(max_dominoes)

solve()
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a set of 21 standard dominoes (with values 1-6, each combination a,b where 1≤a≤b≤6)
2. We have an undirected graph with n vertices and m edges
3. We want to place dominoes on edges such that:
   - Each edge gets at most one domino
   - When placing a domino on an edge (u,v), we choose a direction (u→v or v→u)
   - For any vertex, all domino halves directed toward it must have the same number of dots
4. We want to maximize the number of dominoes placed

## Key Insight

The constraint means that for each vertex, all incoming domino halves must have the same value. This means:
- For vertex v, if it receives domino halves from multiple edges, all those halves must have the same number of dots
- This creates a constraint on how we can assign dominoes to edges

## Approach

This is essentially a constraint satisfaction problem. We can think of it as:
- For each vertex, we need to assign a "value" (from 1 to 6) that all incoming domino halves will have
- When we place a domino on edge (u,v), we choose which endpoint gets which value
- The constraint is that the value assigned to vertex u and vertex v must be consistent with the domino we choose

Since n ≤ 7, we can use brute force or dynamic programming over subsets.

Actually, let me think of this differently:

We can consider each possible assignment of values to vertices. For a given assignment v₁, v₂, ..., vₙ, we can check how many edges we can cover.

For each edge (u,v), we can place a domino on it if and only if there exists a domino with values (v_u, v_v) or (v_v, v_u). Since dominoes are symmetric, we just need one of these pairs to exist.

But there's a catch: we need to ensure that if multiple edges connect to a vertex, they all have the same value at that vertex.

For a given vertex assignment, we can count the number of edges where the required domino exists.

Since n ≤ 7, we can enumerate all possible assignments of values (1-6) to each vertex. There are 6^n possible assignments, which is acceptable since 6^7 = 279,936.

For each assignment:
1. For each edge (u,v), check if domino (min(v_u,v_v), max(v_u,v_v)) exists in the standard set (where 1≤a≤b≤6)
2. Count how many edges can have a domino placed
3. Take the maximum across all assignments

## Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Read edges
edges = []
for _ in range(m):
    u, v = map(int, input().split())
    # Convert to 0-indexed
    edges.append((u-1, v-1))

# Standard domino set: pairs (a,b) where 1 <= a <= b <= 6
# We can represent this as a set of tuples
domino_set = set()
for a in range(1, 7):
    for b in range(a, 7):
        domino_set.add((a, b))

# Function to check if a domino can be placed on edge (u,v) with values at vertices
def can_place_domino(u, v, vertex_values):
    value_u = vertex_values[u]
    value_v = vertex_values[v]
    # The domino needs to have values (min, max) of these two
    a, b = min(value_u, value_v), max(value_u, value_v)
    return (a, b) in domino_set

# Try all possible assignments of values to vertices (each value from 1 to 6)
max_dominoes = 0

# We'll use recursion/backtracking to generate all assignments
from itertools import product

for values in product(range(1, 7), repeat=n):
    # Count how many edges can have a domino placed
    count = 0
    for u, v in edges:
        if can_place_domino(u, v, values):
            count += 1
    max_dominoes = max(max_dominoes, count)

print(max_dominoes)
```

Example 1: n=4, m=4, cycle of 4 vertices. The assignment where all vertices have value 3 would work for all edges, giving 4 dominoes. This matches.

Example 2: n=7, m=0. No edges, so we place 0 dominoes. Correct.

Example 3: n=3, 1 edge between vertices 1-3. We can assign values such that the domino (min, max) exists. For example, if all vertices get value 1, we get domino (1,1) which exists. So we can place 1 domino. Correct.

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Anadi has a set of dominoes. Every domino has two parts, and each part contains some dots. For every a and b such that 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6, there is exactly one domino with a dots on one half and b dots on the other half. The set contains exactly 21 dominoes. Here is an exact illustration of his set:

<image>

Also, Anadi has an undirected graph without self-loops and multiple edges. He wants to choose some dominoes and place them on the edges of this graph. He can use at most one domino of each type. Each edge can fit at most one domino. It's not necessary to place a domino on each edge of the graph.

When placing a domino on an edge, he also chooses its direction. In other words, one half of any placed domino must be directed toward one of the endpoints of the edge and the other half must be directed toward the other endpoint. There's a catch: if there are multiple halves of dominoes directed toward the same vertex, each of these halves must contain the same number of dots.

How many dominoes at most can Anadi place on the edges of his graph?

Input

The first line contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n ≤ 7, 0 ≤ m ≤ (n⋅(n-1))/(2)) — the number of vertices and the number of edges in the graph.

The next m lines contain two integers each. Integers in the i-th line are a_i and b_i (1 ≤ a, b ≤ n, a ≠ b) and denote that there is an edge which connects vertices a_i and b_i.

The graph might be disconnected. It's however guaranteed that the graph doesn't contain any self-loops, and that there is at most one edge between any pair of vertices.

Output

Output one integer which denotes the maximum number of dominoes which Anadi can place on the edges of the graph.

Examples

4

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a set of 21 dominoes where each domino has two parts with dots (a,b) where 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 6
2. We have an undirected graph with n vertices and m edges
3. We want to place dominoes on edges such that:
   - Each edge gets at most one domino
   - When placing a domino on an edge (u,v), we assign direction: one end has value a, other has value b
   - For each vertex, all domino halves facing that vertex must have the same number of dots

## Key Insights

1. The constraint about all halve values at each vertex being the same means that if vertex v has multiple domino halves facing it, they must all have the same value.
2. This creates a dependency: if we decide that vertex v sees value x, then every edge incident to v that gets a domino must have one end equal to x.
3. For an edge (u,v), if we place domino (a,b), then either:
   - a faces u and b faces v, or
   - b faces u and a faces v
4. But the constraint means that all vertices incident to the same edge must have consistent values.

## Approach

This is a constraint satisfaction problem. We can use backtracking with pruning:

1. For each edge, we can choose to place a domino or not.
2. If we place a domino on an edge, we must assign values to both endpoints such that:
   - The values assigned to each vertex are consistent (all halve values at a vertex must be equal)
   - The domino values (a,b) must satisfy the constraint that we're using valid dominoes
